649. WILLIAM LOCKLEY was indicted for feloniously forging, on the 15th of January, a certain request for the delivery of goods, which is as follows:
Please send by the bearer two or three silver watches, as there is a gentleman in our shop wishes to purchase one or two double-bottom cases - send the selling prices, about 3l. or 5l. each. E. COLLINGRIDGE.
Please to send direct.
with intent to defraud Phineas Headworth; against the Statute, &c.
SECOND COUNT, for uttering the same, &c.
PHINEAS HEADWORTH. I keep a watchmaker's shop in St. John-street, St. Sepulchre. On the 15th of January, in the afternoon, the prisoner came and produced this paper; I cannot say whether he said he brought it from Mr. or Mrs. Collingridge - there is a Mr. Collingridge, who is my nephew, who lives in Wilderness-row - he is a watch-case gilder; I sent my son up stairs for some watches, and asked the prisoner if he was to take any-hunting watches; I sent some watches by him, worth about 15l. at the selling prices, I told him so, and he went away with them; I had offered to make a parcel of them, but he said there was no necessity as the distance was so short - this is the paper he brought - I saw the prisoner in about ten days afterwards, at the Half Moon, in Portpool-lane; upon having a full view of him, I was quite sure he was the persen.
Prisoner. After he had looked at me five minutes, he said he was not sure I was the person; the officerdesired me to pull off my hat, and stand against a large window; and then he said if I was not the man. I was very much like him. Witness. I could not speak positively to him at first, but when I saw him in the light I was positive of him.
ALFRED JAMES HEADWORTH. I am a son of the prosecutor. I recollect the prisoner coming to his shop for the watches; I am sure he is the person - I heard him speak at my father's, and I have heard him to-day - I am sure he is the person.
Prisoner He looked at me very hard in the parlour, and said he was not sure. Witness. I was not sure till he was in the light
THOMAS HEADWORTH. I am the prosecutor's son. I came in while the prisoner was at my father's; I am sure he is the person - I went to Mr. Collingridge's, after he was gone to see if they had sent him, and they had not; I went to the Half Moon, and when I saw him in the light, I was sure of him.
RICHARD BAYLIS (Police-constable G 67). I lodge at No. 34, Gray's Inn-lane. I took the prisoner at the Half Moon; Thomas Headworth was there, but he could not tell him till he was in the light - I then sent for the prosecutor and his other son, and as soon as the prisoner got outside the door, they were certain of him- the parlour of the public-house is rather dark; there is a building four or five yards from the window.
EDMUND COLLINGRIDGE. I live at No. 27, Wilderness-row. This note is not my writing, nor my mother's nor sister's; I never sent for the property.
The prisoner put in a written Defence stating that the witnesses were mistaken in his person.
GUILTY of uttering. - Aged 21.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

OLD COURT. WEDNESDAY, FEBRUARY 20TH.
First London Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
650. GEORGE LLOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 3/4 yard of cassimere, value 5s.; 4 gowns, value 8s.; 1 pillow-case, value 6d.; 3 shawls, value 10s.; 4 shifts, value 4s.; 2 night-gowns, value 2s.; 4 aprons, value 2s.; 1 pair of half-boots, value 4s.; 5 caps, value 2s 6d; 5 napkins, value 1s. 6d.; 1 table-cloth, value 1s.; 1 pair of gloves, value 6d.; 1 necklace, value 2s.; 1 snuff-box, value 2s.; 1 pair of ear-rings, value 1s.; 1 tray, value 7s.; 1 watch, value 1l.; 1 seal, value 2d.; 1 watch-key, value 1d.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 4s.; 14 yards of printed cotton, value 7s.; 2 yards of calico, value 8d.; 1 work-box, value 1s., and 1 thimble, 6d., the goods of Timothy Skinner.
TIMOTHY SKINNER. I live at Newbury, Berks, and travel as a hawker of earthenware. On the 10th of February I went to bed about half-past eleven o'clock, and fell asleep; my wife was not in bed with me - the watchman called me up about half-past four - I found the door wide open, and my wife was gone; two children were in bed by the side of me - this property was kept up stairs, in the drawers; I found my wife next day at the Belle Sauvage yard, Ludgate-hill - I found the prisoner sitting in the tap-room, with my wife's bonnet close by his side, and my wife was up stairs. When I awoke, I got a light, looked, and missed my silver watch, and all the articles stated in the indictment - the handkerchiefs were neck-handkerchiefs; I cannot say they were all safe when I went to bed - I know my watch was safe, and several other things; the prisoner lived next door to me - I got to town about seven o'clock in the morning; I found all my property in a room which the prisoner took (No. 11) in the house - it was a bed-room; I found some of my wife's clothes there also - I gave the prisoner in charge for this theft; I had not seen the prisoner the day before - I found all the things in one bundle, except the prisoner's shirt, my wife's shift, and a cap, which was tied up together in another bundle.
WILLIAM ELSTON. I keep the Bell. The prisoner came to the house on Saturday, the 10th of February, with Mrs. Skinner; they got there about two o'clock in the afternoon - the prisoner engaged a room; they slept there on the night of the 10th - it was No. 9, not No. 11; they brought two bundles and a basket as their luggage - I saw Skinner next day, about half-past nine o'clock; he sent for an officer, and charged the prisoner with robbing him; the prisoner said he knew nothing about it - there was a bonnet on the table, and the prosecutor said it was his wife's bonnet - the wife refused to go down to the prosecutor; I went up with him and assisted him in taking thirty-nine sovereigns from her, and half a guinea, which she had taken with her.
GEORGE JOHNSON. I am porter at the Belle Sauvage. I saw the prisoner there on Sunday, the 10th of February, between one and two o'clock; I believe he arrived by a Bristol coach - he was sitting in the tap-room with a female; they had two bundles, and something else there - when I had been there about ten minutes, the prisoner asked if I knew where they could get a bed - I said they might probably be accommodated at the tap, but he took a bed at Elston's, which is the Old Bell - he and Mrs. Skinner both went together.
Prisoner. Q. Did not the woman ask you to get the bed? A. She did.
THOMAS JOHN MORGAN. I live at Newbury. I know the prisoner and Skinner; I understood the prisoner was single - I drive the Monarch Bristol coach from Newbury; it starts off about half-past two o'clock in the morning, and between four and five miles on this side of Newbury, the prisoner hailed me; he had a woman with him, who was afterwards called Mrs. Skinner - I brought them to the Belle Sauvage yard.
WILLIAM STANTON. I am a Policeman. I was sent for and took charge of the prisoner about a quarter before ten o'clock on the 11th of February; I was desired to take him to the Compter; Skinner gave charge of him - I afterwards took the woman; I searched Lloyd at the Compter, and found on him 17s. and a knife - I returned for the woman, and the watch and bundle were given to me; Skinner claimed the contents - I found a shift, a shirt, and cap, in another bundle in the bed-room.(Property produced and sworn to.)
TIMOTHY SKINNER. I used to leave the watch at home for use; I left it with my wife; she left me without anynotice - she had drank a glass of rum and water with me that evening; the articles are mine, I am certain.
Prisoner's Defence. As I was on the road to town. I met with this woman promiscuously; she said she was going to London - I said I was going there; I never asked her business, nor she mine - I did not know she was a married woman; I said I should take the first coach that came by, which would be in half an hour - when it overtook us she asked me to hail it, and we came to London - she said she was an entire stranger, and asked if I objected to her spending the evening with me; I had none of the property - I did not live next door to Skinner; I was discharged from the marines - I have not worked there these ten years; I knew the woman ten years ago, when she was a girl of the town - I thought she ran away from him on account of his ill treatment.
TIMOTHY SKINNER. I have lived with my wife in that house about twelve months; the prisoner lived next door all that time.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330214-222]
651. GEORGE THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January. 1 pair of child's boots, value 2s. 6d., the goods of George Clarke.
WILLIAM TEGG CLARKE. I am the son of George Clarke, who is a shoe-maker, and live in Stonecutter-street. On the 29th of January, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I hung these boots on the door-post, and in about five minutes I saw the prisoner take them off the book - I ran and overtook him, and saw him throw them down; I am certain they were my father's - he went about five yards before he threw them down, on seeing me.
JOHN YATES. I am an officer. I received him in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.
Prisoner. Henry Cook, who was with me, persuaded me to knock them down; I said I would not - he then said do, and I knocked them off for a lark, and he ran away,
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18330214-223]
652. HENRY DALLY was indicted for that he, on the 30th of January, feloniously did offer and utter a certain 5l. Bank-note, well knowing it to be forged, with intent to defraud Joseph Pryor and others; against the Statute.
SAMUEL PRYOR. I am in partnership with Joseph Pryor and others, umbrella-maker s, Holborn-hill. On the 30th of January, about eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to the shop to buy an umbrella; he fixed on one at 13s., and offered me a 5l. note - I looked at it; it appeared to be a bad one - I took it to a neighbour to ask his opinion, which confirmed mine; I then sent one of our men down for an officer - I did not ask the prisoner how he came by it, but gave him in charge for offering it.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Did you see him searched? A. Yes; only 6d. was found on him; the Bank do not prosecute in this case.
JOSEPH DRAKE. I am shopman to Messrs Pryor. The prisoner came on the 30th of January and bought an umbrella for 13s., he produced a note - I asked Mr. Pryor to give him change; Mr. Pryor went into the counting-house and then to a neighbour; in the mean time two more men came into the shop and asked about some umbrellas - The prisoner said I shall not be gone above two minutes, I am putting up at the White Horse, Fetter-lane, and am going off by the York Highflier in the morning; the other men did not speak to him - I let him leave the shop; he returned, and Mr. Pryor charged him with the offence - he had a full opportunity of going away; Mr. Pryor sent me for an officer, who took him - he said he was not aware that it was a bad note.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the note handed to Mr. Pryor? A. Yes; he went out with the note in his hand in the prisoner's presence.
PRYOR CHALLICE. I am waiter at the White Horse, Fetter-Lane. On the 30th of January, between six and seven o'clock the prisoner came there to dine - he left between seven and eight and did not come again; I do not know whether he had taken a place by the York coach - I have nothing to do with the booking-office; he might have taken a place without my knowledge - it leaves our house every morning at nine o'clock.
ISAAC HANNELL. I am a carpenter, and live at Backhill, Hatton Garden. I did not know the prisoner till he was examined at Guildhall - the note has got my name on it; it is not my writing - the note was never in my possession.
JOHN MARCHANT. I am an officer of St. Andrew, Holborn. On the 30th of January I saw the prisoner at the the watch-house, charged with uttering the bad 5l. note; I asked him where he got the note - he refused to tell me where he got it; I asked his name, he told me it was Henry Dally - I asked him where he resided when he was at home; he said, "I shan't tell you" - I asked how he got his living; he said, "I shan't tell you" - the note was delivered to me: I have had it ever since - I found it laying on the desk in the watch-house: Mr. Pryor had marked it.
MR. PRYOR. This is the note - I have put S. P. on it.
ISAAC HANNELL. This is the note; I saw it at the Magistrate's - the address on the back is "Hannell, Hatton Garden;" which is my address - (note read).
JOHN BEAN. I am a watchman. I took the prisoner into custody; the note was handed to me - I put it on the watch-house table after putting my name to it.
JOSHUA FREEMAN. I am inspector of Bank of England notes. This is a forged note in every respect - paper, plate, and signature; it purports to be signed J. D. Carroll, and is not his hand-writing - it is a very bad attempt.
Prisoner's Defence. I took the note in Smithfield market for a horse, which I sold for 7l. 10s.
WILLIAM RICHARD AUSTIN. I am a bricklayer and builder, and live in Union-street, Middlesex-hospital. I was in Smithfield on a Friday in January, I think it was the 25th, and saw the prisoner with a horse; I offered him 6l. 10s. for it - he rather laughed at me; he wanted 10l. - I gave him my card, and told him if he could not make a better price he might call on me; and in a few minutes as I was going to Duke-street, I saw him treating with a man, and he sold it for 7l. 10s. - the man said, "My name is at the back of the note, my name is Hannell;" he gave him a 5l. note and two sovereigns, but I do not know what silver.
COURT. Q. How came you so near them? A. I had beento Duke-street - the person I wanted was dead, and I returned directly; I did not wait with the prisoner five minutes - the horse would have been a bargain at the money I offered; they were in the act of settling for it when I came up - I had no particular reason to notice what money was paid; I did not know the prisoner before - a letter was sent to me about three weeks ago to come as a witness; I left the letter at Guildhall - I did not ask the prisoner where he lived; the Magistrate told me I need not attend any more, but the prisoner's master came and begged me to attend here - I have several houses; I did not see the name of Hannell on the note.
JURY. Q. What colour was the horse? Q. Grey - I sometimes have occasion for two horses, and thought as Spring was coming I should want one.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18330214-224]
653. WILLIAM BAXTER was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 5s., the goods of George Starr; and 1 pair of boots, value 5s., the goods of William Smith .
SECOND COUNT, stating them to be the goods of William Webb.
WILLIAM WEBB. I am porter at the Cross Keys, Wood-street. On Saturday morning, the 16th of February, I was called up about three o'clock, by the watchman, who said a man had come over the gates with a pair of boots: I dressed myself, and came down - two watchmen had hold of the prisoner, who I saw take one pair of boots out of his breeches; they belong to my master, George Starr - I followed them to the watch-house, and they took a pair of boots off his legs, which I identified as belonging to Smith, a lodger; they were in the boot-room up in the gallery.
BENJAMIN JACKSON. I am a watchman. I saw the prisoner coming over the gates of the yard; I stopped him - he had two pairs of boots and a pair of slippers in his jacket; he said they were his own - I rang the bell for the porter, who came down and claimed them; he had no money - the gates are nine feet high.
FRANCIS EARL. I received the prisoner in charge at the watch-house - one pair of boots were on his feet, and the others were produced.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded poverty.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined Three Months.

654. GEORGE WILLIAM BAIN was indicted for putting off a certain forged request for the delivery of goods, well knowing the same to be forged, with intent to defraud Thomas Martin ; against the Statute, &c.
There being no witness to prove that the instrument was forged the prisoner was ACQUITTED.
[trialAccount t18330214-226]
655. GEORGE HENRY MILTON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 seal, value 2l. 10s., the goods of John Banfield.
JOHN BANFIELD. I am a silversmith, and live in Cheapside. On the 11th of February I was called into the shop, about one o'clock; my young man, said the prisoner, who was in the shop, had stolen a seal - I said,"We must search him;" I sent for an officer - he pulled his handkerchief out of his coat pocket, and with it he threw the send on the floor; it has my mark on it - it cost me 2l.
EDWARD FOSTER. I am shopman to Mr. Banfield. - The prisoner came into the shop alone, and asked to look at a few neat gold seals; I showed him some in a tray; I saw a seal in his hand, and missed one from the tray - seeing him keep it in his hand I watched him very closely, and saw him put it into his pocket; he had not asked the price of it - I sent for my master, who came down; I said I suspected he had stolen a seal - he said, "If you think I have robbed you, you may search me," and at the same time drew she handkerchief out of his pocket, and the seal came out with it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Can you take on yourself to say there might not be some mistake? A. Yes - I am quite sure I saw him put it into his pocket - I never said any thing about his having flape to his pocket.
THOMAS DAVIES. I am an officer, and received him in charge.
EDWARD FOSTER. This is one of the seals I showed him.
Prisoner. I am unwilling to add to my crime by denying it; I have hitherto borne a good character, and throw myself on your mercy.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined 14 Days.
[trialAccount t18330214-227]
656. CHARLES CASEY and WILLIAM HENRY TAYLOR were indicted for a conspiracy.
JOHN PHILLIPS. I keep a coffee and chop-house, No. 32, Lime-street. On the 6th of February, about four o'clock in the afternoon, Casey came to me; I had seen him before - he offered me a sample of brandy and hollands; I approved of it, and was to give him 15s. a gallon - I ordered two gallons of each, and two two-gallon casks came to me, in consequence of the order, about seven o'clock the same evening; Casey and some stranger brought them - it was not Taylor; they took the casks into the coffee-room: Casey had one cask tied in a handkerchief, and the other had the other cask in a bag; they were placed in the coffee-room - I was to pay for them then; they came to 3l. - when they brought them, I desired my waiter to laste it, and he said the spirit was very good, but it was very thick and muddy, and I declined having any thing to do with it, but the other man said he had had it a long time, and I should have it for 2l., which I paid for it; at that time I believed the casks were full of liquor - I went down stairs to pay them; Casey said, "You will pay my friend, it will be all the same;" I paid his friend; they directly went away, and in about three minutes I examined the casks; I drew the hung, and put a gallon can under them; about half a pint run out of each cask into the can - I immediately got a ginblet, and bored a hole in the head of each cask, and dirty water came from it; there was rather more than four gallons of water and spirits together - I afterwards examined the casks, and found a bottle was fixed to the nose of the cask, and was fixed to the bung so that the spirits would come out at the bung, but not the water; I immediately gave information to the Police - I understood Casey was living at No. 14, White Lion-street, Norton-falgate; I sawCasey on the 14th, by Moorfields chapel, standing by a public-house, and I gave him in charge of my waiter while I went for an officer, and when I returned I found he had got away; he was secured the same day; I saw Taylor when we took Casey, but he made his escape.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You were well acquainted with Casey? A. No, I had seen him about six times; he was introduced to me; I did not know him as a commission-agent - he did not say it was not his liquor: we were half an hour bargaining for it.
Taylor. Q. Did not you call at my house on the evening you bought the liquor? A. Yes, and saw your wife - I called in White Lion-street; I understood both the prisoners lived there; his wife told me some blackguard had been there.
WILLIAM MERRITT. I am in the employ of Mr. Phillips. I did not see Casey when the samples were brought; I tasted them, they appeared very good - I only tasted the brandy sample; in about an hour or an hour and a half I saw Casey at master's, with a lusty man - it was not Taylor; they brought two kegs, each holding two gallons - I heard it was to be 15s. a gallon, but the Hollands was thick; Casey and the other man said they should be there in three weeks, when they would call for the kegs, and Phillips might pay them what he liked then - he gave them 2l.; immediately after they left, I went to measure it - I put a gallon can under each keg; the bunghole only ran half a pint from each - I shook the casks, and each felt quite full; we bored a hole, and to our great surprise, a great deal of dirty water came out of each cask - all the rest was dirty water; we opened the casks, found a pint-bottle was fixed to the bunghole, and glued round - the water could not affect the spirits; on the 14th I saw Casey at the bottom of Broker's-row, standing facing the Brown Bear - master asked if I knew him; I said "Yes, that is the man who sold the spirits" - I went up and collared him, while master went for an officer; Casey said, "Don't let me stand in the street, walk into the public-house, which I did;" Taylor was there, and said,"What do you do holding that man; does he owe you any thing?" I said "No, but he has committed a great fraud on my master" - "What," said Taylor, "I suppose you are vexed because you have been tricked: but we shall trick you again, if you don't mind;" he pulled out two pint-bottles, one out of each pocket - he drew the cork of one, and asked the landlord for a glass; he poured out what appeared to be brandy - the landlord said it was very good; he put the bottle into his pocket, then laid hold of Casey, and said, "Come along with me, and I dare that fellow to follow you" - I said, "If I can't hold you, I shall follow you;" I followed him with the intention of giving him in charge to the first officer I met - Taylor told me, about the middle of Broad-street-buildings, that if I laid my hand on him, he would knock me down; I saw master and an officer coming, and then collared Casey, and Taylor ran away.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was the money paid to? A. Casey; the Bear is about half a mile from Lime-street.
JOHN ISAACS. I am a wine porter in the service of Mr. Charles James, of Fenchurch-street. On the evening of the 7th I was at the Lamb Coffee-house, kept by Phillips; the prisoner Taylor was there - I am quite certain of him- Phillips was not in the way; Taylor asked Mrs. Phillips if Mr. Phillips was at home - she said No; he said he would wait until he came in: he sat down, had some ale and a pipe, and told me he had put them up to the scheme of selling the brandy to Phillips; I had called at his house with Phillips the night before, at No. 14 White Lion-street; he asked me what business I had to come there, and make a noise; I told him we made no noise, that Phillips sent there to inquire after Casey - he said he had put them up to it; that he should not have known that he sold it to Phillips if they had not gone there the night they sold it, and insulted his wife; he said he went to the public-house that the party who was not in custody used, and had put up three half-pints of gin to his score; he said that the casks cost 6s. - he left before Phillips came home.
Taylor. Q. Was not I smoking a pipe with the ale before me before you came in? A. No, he had been drinking, and was not quite sober, or he might not have said this: he knew what he said I should think.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you introduced Casey to the prosecutor? A. No; I knew him before - I never knew him sell on commission.
PETER KELLY. I am an officer. I apprehended Casey on the 14th, in Liverpool-street - Phillips charged him with felony; I detained him - he said he hoped he should not be charged with felony, and walked quietly away with me - I took Taylor in Guildhall-yard; he had been to visit Casey in the Compter, and walked with him from there to Guildhall - he said he was Casey's master; I said, "I hope you will come and get your servant out of trouble?" he said he would, but when he got to the corner he started off - I told Phillips, and in about two hours I saw him behind a chariot, and secured him.
Taylor. When he came to the Compter he found me there: I said Casey was selling goods for me, and if he was in trouble I would walk with him to Guildhall - I went and waited outside - he said, "Walk in with me," and the Magistrate asked if I had been seen in the transaction till he had apprehended Casey - he said not; the Magistrate said he should bind me over as a witness, and afterwards that he would hold us to bail; I knew nothing of it till after it occurred - I went home about eleven o'clock; my wife said a person had been there about some brandy - on the following day I found Phillips lived in Lime-street, and went there to have an explanation; I waited two hours for him, and as it was getting late I returned - on the 14th, when Casey was given in charge, I said, "Does he owe you any thing, for if he does I will pay you?" he said No, he had defrauded his master; I said, "Have you any authority to take him?" he said No; I said, "Well, we may as well walk on towards home then," and in the street, the officer came and took him into custody - instead of my running away as they say, I went to the Bear, and waited two hours, thinking it might be settled.
CASEY - GUILTY. Aged 27.
TAYLOR - GUILTY. Aged 38.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330214-228]
NEW COURT. WENDESDAY, FEBRUARY 20TH.
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
657. JAMES MOORE was indicted for stealing, onthe 14th of February, 1 piece of cloth, value 6d.; 1 wrench, value 3s.; 1 patent cutter, value 3s.; 2 head-knuckles, value 2s. 6d.; 10 plates, value 1s.; 3 hinges, value 3d.; 100 screws, value 6d.; 64 bolts, 2s.; 100 brads, value 6d.; 2 knives, value 1s.; 1 file, value 1s., and 1 fork, value 6d., the goods of Samuel Hoadley.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the prosecution.
SAMUEL HOADLEY. I am a coach-maker; my manufactory is in Hind-mews - the prisoner was eighteen months in my employ. On the 14th of February, I got an officer, and went to his house; I there found this piece of cloth, which I know to be mine - the prisoner denied it, and said he had bought it in Oxford-street, but afterwards he said he thought it was mine - that he had put into his pocket to do a job to some nobleman's carriage, and had forgotten to take it out of his pocket again; but he had no business with it, as he is not employed in that way - I found this patent washer-cutter there and some other things; this cutter has my initials on it - I found this file, these brads, and other articles; I found this wrench, which he acknowledged to be mine; he said he had had it upwards of six months to do a job, and had forgotten to return it - these two knives and a fork have the name of Gurney, of Maidstone, on them, and I bought them there by chance - the value of the property is about 100l.
COURT. Q. Might he have had these things for use, and not returned them? A. I found part of my property working up for other persons; he ought not to have worked for other persons - he told me he was satisfied with his wages, and did not wish to work over-time.
ROBERT CROWE (Police-serjeant D). I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. This washer-cutter my master gave me to use, and said if I lost it he should expect another made - I could not use it, and he got me a new one made; I took the wrench in my pocket on the Saturday before to work at the other shop; I took it to my house to take the next morning - I took the piece of cloth to finish a gentleman's carriage, and there is grease on it now; I did not say I bought it, my wife said she thought I had - these brads and screws are what I bought before I went to the prosecutor; I was out of a situation four months, and worked for different stable-keepers, in repairing carriages.
MR. HOADLEY. He was a coach-maker's labourer, not a coach-maker; I should not think he could repair carriages; he stated that he bought near a thousand nails and brads at the same place where he said he bought this file, but they do not sell files there; I lost some thousands of screws in whole papers, and these papers were found at his house.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330214-229]
658. WILLIAM ANDERSON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of January, 2 shirts, value 14s.; 1 gown, value 10s., and 1 petticoat, value 2s., the goods of Elizabeth Campbell.
ELIZABETH CAMPBELL. I live in Whitmore-street; I am a laundress. I lost a shirt from my copper on the 14th of January, and the officer brought it to me in the course of three days; this is it - I have had it constantly to wash ever since it was made; the mark has been cut out, and a piece put in the place - the prisoner lodged next door to me.
JOSEPH MELLISH (Police-constable N 5). I apprehended the prisoner at the Robin Hood public-house; I found this shirt on him - he said he bought it in Chiswell-street, and then in Chick-lane.
Prisoner's Defence. I said I bought it in Chick-lane.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18330214-230]
660. WILLIAM ANDERSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 2 shifts, value 2s.; 2 petticoats, value 3s.; 1 gown, value 4s., and 1 apron, value 8d., the goods of Catherine Whitaker.
THOMAS BRETT (Police-constable N 139). I was in the Green-lanes on the 2nd of February; I stopped the prisoner with a bundle, and asked him what he had got - he said he did not know; I asked where he was going to take it - he said to Mr. Clifton, salesman, Newgate-market, and he had brought it from Mr. Mattherson's, at Enfield, where he said he was ostler, and had been so four months; I took him - in going to the station he jumped away; I jumped after him - I fell down, and lost him; he was taken on Wednesday evening.
CATHERINE WHITAKER. These are my things; I lived at Mr. Mattherson's - the prisoner did not live there, but was- post boy at Mr. Ryder's, and used to come there every night, and go back every morning.
Prisoner's Defence. A young man overtook me with this bundle, and asked if I was going to London; I said I was - he asked me to take this to the Peacock, at Islington for him; he said, "I live at the George Inn, Enfield"- I said, "I do not think you do; I was there five or six months ago, and you did not live there then" - he said that person was gone away, and he said, "If you will take it, I will give you sixpence;" I said I should be stopped, and so I was.
THOMAS BRETT. The parcel was directed.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330214-231]
659. REBECCA FISHER was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 23 yards of lace, value 12s., and eight handkerchiefs, value 6s., the goods of Daniel Stubbs.
JAMES CROSBY. I am shopman to Mr. Daniel Stubbs, haberdasher, Church-street, Hackney. On the 14th of February the prisoner came and asked for some pillow-lace, for children's caps: she looked at that, and then asked for some handkerchiefs - I watched her, and told her I had suspicion of her; I opened her cloak, and found eight handkerchiefs under her arm - this piece of lace dropped from her lap; she said she had nothing.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was she not sitting down? A. Yes; she might have gone away - I was serving a customer; the prisoner was sitting on the lefthand side of the shop, and I was serving on the right, my face was towards her - she had asked Mrs. Stubbs for the lace, and then for the handkerchiefs, but Mrs. Stubbs had just then asked some ladies to take a seat; she was about half a yard from the door.
JANE MATTHEWS. I am the wife of David Matthews. On the afternoon of the 14th of February, I went to the shop - the prisoner came after I was there; Mrs. Stubbs said she would serve her directly - she asked for somelace; there was none to suit her - she then asked for some handkerchiefs; she showed her some cotton ones - she said No, she wanted silk; some ladies came in - Mrs. Stubbs asked them to sit down, and the prisoner took a seat; I saw her draw something into the arm-hole of her cloak, but I could not see what it was - before I had an opportunity of telling the shopman, he went and took the handkerchiefs from under her arm, and the lace dropped down.
Cross-examined. Q. Did this child make the least attempt to go away? A. No, she was waiting to look at some silk shawls.
CHARLES DEWING TYLER (Police-serjeant N 10). I was on duty at the watch-house, and the prisoner was given into my custody - I produce the articles.
Cross-examined. Q. There is no great bulk in them? A. No, she might have escaped with them.
JAMES CROSBY. These are my master's handkerchiefs and lace.
Cross-examined. Q. She was searched in the parlour? A. Yes, Sir; I was not present - I heard that half a crown was found on her.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not intend to take them - I was standing or leaning on them, and I did not know it.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18330214-232]
661. JAMES EAST was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February. 1 half-crown, and 15 shillings, the monies of Robert Keith, from his person.
ROBERT KEITH. I was originally a Custom-house officer - I live in Brewer-street, Golden-square. On the 14th of February I was carrying a loaf home - when I got to my own door I put down my umbrella to apply my key to the door - the umbrella opened again, and while I was attending to that, and trying to open the door, the prisoner came and offered to turn the key of the door for me - I let go my hand that he might turn it, as I knew him; he then put his hand into my pocket, and took out a little bag, with fifteen shillings and one half-crown in it; I did not speak to him, I only said, "Halloo, you scoundrel;" nor did he speak to me - I had the money safe just before.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. How long have you known him? A. Since the 29th of June; there was no person near us - it was about half-past eight o'clock in the evening; I did not raise an alarm - he pulled out the purse, and 4s. 6d. in sixpences flew out of it, into the passage - I picked them up: I had been at the Court of Requests the day before, and the prisoner spoke to me then; my son had a claim on him for 17s. rent - I was to call on him that day fortnight, and he said he would pay me, but I had no quarrel with him - I conceived he was a poor man, and not able to pay.
CHARLES DEWING (Police-constable C 1.) I apprehended the prisoner at a public-house in Macclesfield-street, Soho - I had received information a little before ten o'clock, and left word for him to come; I found on him 7l. 5s. 5 1/2d.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he is a butcher? A. Yes; I have known him some months - he has a shop at the corner of Newport-market; I had seen him about ten o'clock on the night of the robbery, but I did not take him, as I did not know of whom the complaint was made.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in my own shop at the time he says he was robbed.
HENRY WOODS. I am a journey man butcher. The prisoner is a master butcher, and cuts meat in the front of a shop at the corner of Newport-market - on the 14th of February I saw him in his shop from between seven and eight o'clock till between eight and nine: I saw him for three-quarters of an hour, till very near half-past eight - he then left with his brother.
COURT. Q. How far is Brewer-street from there? A. About a quarter of a mile; I work in the same shop as the prisoner; Mr. Warren, my master, cuts at one front, and the prisoner at the other.
WILLIAM EAST. I am the prisoner's brother. I was at his shop that evening; he shut up and left his shop with me a little before nine o'clock - I had been there all the afternoon; he came between seven and eight - we left a little before nine o'clock, and went to Mr. Green's, a butcher, in Crown-court, about five minutes' walk from Newport-market; we did not go into Mr. Green's shop, but stood at the door - I have a brother named Alfred who lives there, and we went to tell him of the death of my brother Frederick; we stood at the door a few minutes.
COURT. Q. How far is that from Brewer-street? A. I do not know Brewer-street, nor the prosecutor's; my brother died the same afternoon, about three o'clock - he was buried on Sunday, at St. Pancras church.
JOSEPH KIRBY. I am landlord of the house the prisoner was taken from - I have known him near twelve months - he has had a very good character.
COURT. Q. Where did he lodge? A. I do not know, but on the afternoon of the 14th of February he came to my house, between four and five o'clock; he said he had lost a brother; he remained there till about half-past seven o'clock, and then went away - my house is about a hundred and fifty yards from his shop; he appeared to be distressed - I saw him again about ten o'clock that evening; I was not aware he was in the house then till the officer came - I then went up, and told the prisoner the officer wanted him; he came down, and went alone to the station-house; he came back, and was taken about half-past twelve o'clock - his shop is about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's.
ROBERT KEITH. There was a very good light opposite my door, and I had a light in my passage; when he stooped down his face was close to me - I cannot be mistaken in him.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18330214-233]
662. WILLIAM PUMMELL and RICHARD NEWMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of January, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of a person unknown, from his person.
THOMAS TOOL. I am a shoemaker, and live in Whitecross-place. On Thursday night, the 31st of January, I was in Church-street, about six o'clock; I saw the prisoners following two gentlemen - I watched them, and when they got to Hackney-road I saw Pummell take a handkerchief out of one of the gentlemen's pockets, and give it to Newman; I told the gentleman, and said, "Thatis the person, lay hold of him;" Newman ran down a street or two, and I pursued him till he got to a street which is not a thoroughfare, and there I took him with the handkerchief in his hand; he begged hard of me to let him go, said he would give me 2s., and asked me to have something to drink - after some time I got him near the corner where the handkerchief had been stolen; I there met an officer, and gave him to him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you recollect what my Lord my said to you in last February Session? A. No, I do not recollect being here, but I might have been; I have been here several times - I do not know whether I have been here twenty times; I do not recollect prosecuting two men for stealing a handkerchief of John Keats ' last February Session, but it might be so.
Q. Upon your solemn oath, did you not swear you saw one Johnson take the handkerchief from Mr. Keat's pocket and put it into his breeches; and after thus swearing, did not the Jury acquit the prisoners, and my Lord tell you never to show your face here again? A. I do not recollect it; I remember the name of Henry Johnson and David Wells - I recollect swearing that I saw Johnson put his hand into Mr. Keat's pocket and take out his handkerchief, and put it into his breeches; but the Jury acquitted the prisoner, because you stifled the case; I began to be a witness seven or eight years ago, in consequence of my father losing some shoes - I have prosecuted persons once or twice for taking handkerchiefs from persons unknown - I will swear it is not five times; I did not get my expences last time - I did not apply for them; I have always been praised by Judges and Magistrates - I did not get my expences, because you stifled the case altogether: I never think about expences - I suppose in the last twelve months I have got 10s. or 12s. - I may have got 2l. in twelve months.
JAMES THOMPSON (Police-constable H 78).) I received Newman from Tool, and he pointed out Pummell to me, as I returned from Worship-street.
Newman's Defence. Two boys dropped the handkerchief; I took it up and ran across the road, and Tool took me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18330214-234]
663. JANE CLARK was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 1 jacket, value 3s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 1s. 6d., the goods of John Sharp.
HENRY LEE (Police-constable K 262.) On the night of the 28th of January, I went to No. 5, Sun-court; I found the prisoner there - I asked her what she had done with the jacket, trousers, and waistcoat: she said she had given them to a man to sell, and she did not know where they were sold - I went with her to Hyam's, who said he had bought these clothes; Shelly was sent for, and he stated that the prisoner gave him the clothes to sell - he has been sent on board a shop, he was a deserter; the prosecutor gave his name as John Sharp before the Magistrate, but he is now gone to sea.
MARY ANN HOWARD. I keep a house No. 2, Union-street. The prisoner came there with a man on the 28th of January; I was called up into the room between nine and ten o'clock - the prisoner was gone; I found the man without his clothes, he had no clothes to put on, and I lent him my husbands; he stated before the Magistrate that his name was John Sharp.
HENRY HYAM. I am a slop-seller, and live at No. 137, High-street, Shadwell. I know a man who went by the name of Shelly, he brought me these clothes, and I bought them for 2s.; the prisoner came in about a quarter of an hour afterwards, and asked if a man had sold some clothes there - I said Yes, for 2s.; she said, I want them back, "and she threw down 2s.;" but I would not let her have them, as I thought the man had sold them to pay her the money.
GUILTY. Aged 21. Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18330214-235]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
664. ROBERT COLLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 8 bottles, value 2s.; 1 1/2 pint of rum, value 3s., and 4 quarts of whiskey, value 1l., the goods of Sophia Lash Tarbutt and others.
SOPHIA LASH TARBUTT. I am in partnership with my four sisters - we keep the Royal Hotel, in Leicester-square - the prisoner was our porter for about five weeks In consequence of information I went into the kitchen, on the 12th of February; I saw a box belonging to him in a closet, I found in it seven bottles of whiskey, and one of rum; I have reason to believe it was ours, as we had a hogshead of whiskey in the cellar - there are lahels on two of the bottles, which I know; the officer asked the prisoner why he took it, and he said to distribute amongst his friends; he did not say where from.
Cross-examined. Q. What name is over the door? A. Elizabeth Harris; our license is in that name - my sisters and I divide the profits between us; the box was not locked.
CHARLES YOUNG (Police-constable C 140). I was sent for, and went into the kitchen; I saw this liquor found in the box - the prisoner came in; I asked him how he came possessed of so much liquor; he said he drew it from a cask in the cellar that he was sorry he had done it, and he intended to distribute it among his friends.
SOPHIA LASH TARBUTT. These are our bottles; the cellar is kept locked, and the key is in the bar - one of my sisters draws the liquor; the prisoner has nothing to do with the cellar, we suppose he must have taken the key in the night.
GUILTY. Aged 23.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18330214-236]
665. ELIZABETH BAKER was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 tea-caddy, value 3s., the goods of Charles Danieli.
CHARLES PIERCE. On the 14th of February I was in Oxford-street, and saw the prisoner take a teacaddy from a board fixed outside a shop, and walk away; I told the prosecutor - his shopman followed her, and took her.
WILLIAM VAN (Police-constable D 94). The prisoner was given to me; I found no money on her, but two breasts of mutton, five duplicates, a book, and two pairs of ladies shoes, quite new, with the ticket on them,"3s. 6d. a pair."
JAMES DALY. I am shopman to Mr. Charles Danieli, a jeweller - he sells caddies; this is his caddy - the witness gave me information; I took the prisoner thirty yards off, with this caddy under her shawl, secreted.
Prisoner's. Defence. I was in great distress, and in an ill state of health
GUILTY. Aged 34. Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18330214-237]
666. SOPHIA BIRCH was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 13th of February, 90 pieces of timber, value 10l.; 30 slates, value 2s.; 20 lbs. weight of lead, value 3s., and 1000 bricks, value 30s., the goods of Charles Thorogood, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and ROWE conducted the prosecution.
CHARLES THOROGOOD. I have been a builder. I have some carcases of houses in the neighbourhood of the Alpha-cottages, Regent's-park; I never gave the prisoner permission to take any thing from them - I do not know her.
EDWARD THOMAS. I am a carpenter. I live in Earl-street, Lisson-grove, near the Alpha-cottages; I went to work close by the prisoner's - I know the carcases of houses which are close by the prosecutor's garden-wall, within fourteen or fifteen yards; I saw the prisoner's son in the yard of those carcases, and a great many more boys on the tops of the houses, tearing down the pots from the chimneys, then the bricks, and throwing them over the wall into the yard of the prisoner's cottage - the prisoner's son was throwing the bricks from the yard of these carcases into the prisoner's garden.
ANDREW DELLANEY. I am a shoemaker, and live in Bridport-street, opposite the prisoner. I know these five carcases in front of her house - I have seen boys pulling down the bricks: the greatest depredation was in January; but recently I have seen timber carried by the back entrance into her cottage-garden, by lads - the pieces seemed exactly the same as these which are here.
ROBERT HARRISON. I am a bricklayer, and live in Dorchester-place, very near the Alpha-cottages, and near the prisoner's house. I have seen boys on the carcases of the houses in question, pulling down the bricks, and other boys were throwing them towards the prisoner's garden, where I saw a bricklayer at work, building a wall.
COURT. Q. You have seen two or three boys who have gone out of Court? A. Yes, and one of them I can swear to as one of the boys; he is the tallest of them - I do not know his name.
CHRISTOPHER BUNKELL. I am a builder - I live in Dorchester-place, and am agent to the ground landlord of these carcases. I went over these five carcases one day last week - I suppose they have sustained damage to the amount of 200l. and upwards; joists, bricks, and slates, have been taken away, the chimney-pots destroyed, and the carcases greatly demolished - I know where the prisoner lives; there is a door from her garden to the back part of these carcases - I went to her house with two boys last Wednesday; I saw the prisoner there, and said, "I understood you are encouraging these boys to destroy these houses, and loosen the timbers - I am credibly informed there is a quantity of the materials in your cottage;" she said there was no property in her house belonging to any one but herself - I told her I was confident there was, and I was going to get an officer with a search-warrant, unless the property was delivered up; I left a man to watch the premises - I went away, and returned the same day with the officer; I then told her, I should, as a friend, wish her to give up the timber belonging to these houses - she said, "Come in, Sir;" I walked in with the officer as far as the parlour-door; I then said, "Mrs. Birch, you have got a good deal of the property belonging to these houses, and I am authorised by the proprietor to require you to give it up;" she said, "O deary me, we have nothing belonging to these houses" - I turned and looked into the other parlour on the left hand, and saw, I suppose, fifty or sixty pieces of timber piled up - or probably more; I said, "This is the timber we are looking for" - she said "O no, I gave either 2l. 3s. or 3l. 3s. for them, and a load of bricks;" I said, "Who did you buy it of? you must have had a great burgain" - she said, "I bought it of Mr. Morand;" I said, "What, the broker" - she said,"Yes, he is a broker;" I suppose the value of a load of bricks and the timber I saw would be 10l. or 12l. - the bricklayer came in at the time, and she said, "Here is a man who can prove I had them of Mr. Morand; and I had some words about paying the balance of the money, and he saw it come;" she said to him, "Did not you, Mr. Milwood; did not you see it come" - he said, "I saw a load of bricks and a piece or two of timber;" I said, "Did you see all this?" - he said, No; I asked the prisoner if she had any receipt, and she said it should be given at a proper time; I said we should persevere, and she said we might do as we pleased - I begged of her repeatedly to give it up, but she refused; I said there would certainly be a prosecution follow, as the property belonged to these premises - she said it did not; I then went to my employer, and then went to the prisoner's premises with the officer, and found a large quantity of wood, I believe ninety-four pieces in the whole, also a small piece of lead worth about 2s., and some slates - I compared the timber found at the prisoner's with the remainder of the joists left on the buildings; it matched in length and size, and its being faulty at one end - it tallied in every respect, and I have no doubt whatever that it came from there - here are two pieces I can speak to more particularly; I have matched one piece to the place it came from in the carcases, and it matched to the greatest nicety - the ends of these pieces have been cut off and rubbed with dirt; I have seen boys on the carcases from time to time, (and among the rest, Barnes,) throwing bricks into the prisoner's garden.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How many times did you go to the prisoner's cottage? A. Three times, and the first time I told her the charge, and wished her to give it up, which she refused to do; I first went on the Wednesday, and then again on the Thursday - I am aware that at auctions property is bought for much less than at shops.
MR. ROWE. Q. Did the prisoner ever state that she purchased these goods at an auction? A. No, she did not.
FRANCIS KEYS. I am an officer of Marylebone. I went with this witness, on Friday morning last, to search the prisoner's house; I read my warrant to her, and told her she was in custody, and I was going to take her and the whole of the timber away - I had seen the timber there the day before, when I went to apprehend one of the persons who is to give evidence, and she then said she did not know any such person; I found more than ninety pieces of timber in her house, there was a whole van load - I examined it, and found about half of the ends had been sawed off in a roughish manner; it had been newly done, but some dirt had been rubbed over it to make it appear old;. I said to a person who was with me, that I supposed there was not much pigeon left on the tops of the houses, and the prisoner said, "Oh, deary me, I have got a piece of lead; I heard a great noise between four and five o'clock one morning; I got up, went to one of the houses, and found a piece of lead, which I have in the house" - I recollected that I had seen a piece of lead on a range, but it was then missing - I found it stuffed behind a grate; I found is a cupboard, a number of bits of wood split up - I said, "Mrs. Birch, what is this?" she said, "Only a little fire-wood, and the back of an old cupboard;" I said, "I shall take it away;" she put her hands on me, and said,"Oh, don't take my fire-wood away, it is only wood I have got to light my fire;" I said, "I shall take it, and keep your hands off, or it will be the worse for you;" this is it; when I went and said I came to take her and the timber, she said, "O Lord, what a piece of work there is about this timber - I bought it of a man of the name of Morand, and paid for it; I gave 3l. 3s. for it, and a quantity of bricks" - I said, "You had a very cheap bargain, have you any bill?" she pretended to look among some papers for it, but could not find it - she said she did not know where the man lived, but she afterwards told me where he had lived; I saw part of this timber fitted to the carcases - it matched according to my judgment.
Cross-examined. Q. She told you where Morand lived? A. Yes, but I went, and he had been dead nine weeks.
CHRISTOPHER BUNKELL. I took the two boys on the Wednesday, but as I did not press any charge against them, they were discharged.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you happen to know that the prisoner is under a covenant to lay out money on her house? A. No.
WILLIAM BARNES. I know the carcases at the back of the prisoner's house; I have been on them with the prisoner's son, carrying the wood out of these carcases into the prisoner's cottage - it was put into her kitchen; the prisoner was there, and I saw her daughter sawing off the ends of the wood we carried in - and the prisoner dirtied the ends with some dirt brought from the garden; I cannot tell how many times we took wood there, it was perhaps ten times.
WILLIAM BOWLER. I was at the prisoner's on the Wednesday before she was taken - I was employed to hold the wood while they sawed it; the ends they sawed off were chopped up into small pieces like these.
Cross-examined. Q. Who chopped it? A. The other boy - Mrs. Birch employed him to do it.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the wood of Morand for 3l. 3s., and 11s. for the bricks - I understood I was to have them free of cartage, but I had to pay for it; I was short of money, and did not pay him - he said he would bring a bill, and I was to pay him; I never employed either of the boys - they have had a bribe to appear against me; Thomas has been turned off our premises for committing nuisances, when the bricks and timber came to my house, I called Millwood, and asked him to come and unload, as I had no one to do it; he came and took the bricks, but he said he was using compo', and could not unload the timber - he said he did not think I had had a good bargain, for it was most of it decayed; we had had the sweeps on the Monday morning, and I suppose they put the lead down to keep the soot from the bricks - I have heard that the boys have thrown bricks over my premises, and I have had four sash squares broken; I have begged them to go away - the building next to us shakes with the wind, and the boys took the main rafter from them last week, and I begged them to go away; they brought some wood to my daughter - she would not buy it; they said they would make a good fire with it; I only employed the boy to cut up an old cupboard.
MR. CLARKSON to WILLIAM BARNES. Q. Did you receive money from the prisoner from time to time? A. Yes, for taking the wood into the house.
JAMES MILLWOOD. I am a bricklayer, and have been occasionally employed by the prisoner - I remember a load of timber and bricks coming to her in November; I was at that time working for Mr. Griffiths - I ran over but as my labourer was mixing compo', I could not stop; I heard the prisoner arguing with the men at the gate, about 3s. they had overcharged for coming - on another occasion I saw a quantity of timber go in a cart; my brother was with me then measuring a foundation - I understand the prisoner has her cottage on a repairing lease.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you in the habit of frequenting that neighbourhood? A. I live within a short distance of it; I know there are some carcases - I do not know how many; I was the person who carried up the wall in her garden - the boys were not throwing bricks over into the garden while I was there; the wall I built was composed of old and new bricks - I should think four or five thousand new bricks was delivered from time to time; I do not know of any of the joints of the carcases being taken away - I have not received any money from the prisoner since she has been in custody; I did not notice whether the timber which came in the cart was such as these - I cannot tell whether the cart had one or two horses: I did not notice the same that was on it - I should not know the man who drove it; it was a fairish cart - it was bricks that came when there was the haggling about the money - I saw some old doors among the timber that came; there were some old doors - I cannot say whether there were any slates or not.
HENRY MILWOOD. I am a bricklayer. I worked for the prisoner, in building a wall; I know the five carcases, they have been standing seven or eight years; I found the bricks on the premises, which I used in building the wall - a great many of them had been used before; I did not notice whether they were such as the carcases were built with - there are a great manyboys playing about there; but I know nothing about bricks being pulled down - I saw wood and bricks brought to the prisoner's house in a fairish sized cart, a rubbish cart; the person who brought it, does jobbing for bricklayers - I did not notice the colour of the horse, there were two at one time, and one the other - I do not know where the timbers were put in the prisoner's house - I was at my work in the back yard - the cart came by the street; I do not know any thing of the driver - I know there was a man making a dispute about some money; he was a tallish thin man - I did not notice his dress; it was some time in November - I never saw a single brick thrown from the houses while I was there.
MR. CLARKSON to ROBERT HARRISON. Q. Did you see this witness ? A. I did; he was building the wall while the boys were throwing the bricks over .
GUILTY. Aged 64. Recommended to Mercy.
Confined Six Months , without Labour.
[trialAccount t18330214-238]
667. MARIA BRYANT was indicted for stealing , on the 27th of January, 8s., the monies of Francis William Herbrechter, from his person.
FRANCIS WILLIAM HERBRECHTER. I am a coachsmith. On the 27th of January I was in Boyd's-place , Shoreditch, at half-past two o'clock in the morning ; I had been spending the evening at a friend's at Hoxton, and as I was going by the end of Church-street, Shoreditch, the prisoner accosted me; she told me a young man who was standing there was after no good - I proceeded on to the next turning, and then she came and wanted me to go home with her, which I refused; she then put her hand into my waistcoat pocket and took out my money - she went off; the officer was going by the top of the turning - he asked what I had lost; I told him, and he ran and took her with the money in her month .
WILLIAM HENRY CLARK ( Police-constable H 34). The prosecutor charged her with robbing him of 8s.; I took her and found she had something in her mouth, and I found it was this 8s .
Prisoner's Defence . I am innocent ; I did not mention any thing about any man .
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

668. JAMES MAY was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 1 book, value 10s., the goods of John Brooks.
JOHN BRICKHILL. I had information that a Bible had been stolen; on the 25th of January the prisoner came to our house, about twelve o'clock, and brought this Bible - I asked him his name, and where he lived; he said Evans , No. 11, Warner-street - I went there, but no such person lived there; he said he had had the book twenty years , and Brodie, of Spring-gardens, could answer that .
JOHN BROOKS. I am a carpenter. This is my Bible ; I lost it from No. 9, Blandford-square on the 23rd of January , from the front room; I do not know the prisoner .
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that a young man whom he saw bring the Bible out of a shop, gave it to him to pledge .
WILLIAM EVANS. I am the father of the prisoner. On the Wednesday when this book is represented to have been stolen, he was at home till eleven o'clock at night .
NOT GUILTY.

669. JOHN JENKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 4 lbs. of pork , value 2s., the goods of George Richardson.
GEORGE RICHARDSON. I am a cheesemonger, and live in High-street , Shadwell. About ten o'clock on the evening of the 2nd of February, the officer brought the prisoner to my shop ; I missed a hand of pork - it was produced : I have not the least doubt it was mine .
ERASMUS CHARLTON ( Police-constable K 133). I saw the prisoner and another boy looking in at the prosecutor's window - the prisoner put his hand in, and took the hand of pork .
GUILTY Aged 14. - Whipped and Discharged

670. WILLIAM HENDON was indicted for stealing , on the 6th of February, 1 handkerchief , value 3s ., the goods of Benjamin Dickison, from his person.
BENJAMIN DICKISON. I was in the New-road on the 6th of February , between eleven and twelve o'clock in the day - I did not feel my handkerchief taken, but a man apprised me of it; I turned, and saw it in the middle of the road - the prisoner was pointed out to me , and I seized him.
JAMES PARTRIDGE. I was going from Somer's-town , on the 6th of February , about twelve o'clock in the day ; I saw the prisoner and two others take hold of this gentleman's pocket; one of them took the handkerchief and gave it to the prisoner ; I seized him - he dropped it, and I took him to the office , he said he did not take it.
GUILTY. Aged 18 - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18330214-242]
671. JOHN WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of February, 24 lbs. of beef , value 12s., the goods of Henry Howes.
HENRY HORWOOD. I am servant to Mr. Henry Howes; he is a butcher, and lives in Brook's-market. On the 2nd of February I was at home , but did not see this meat taken ; I was told of it, and then missed some ribs of beef; I ran into Leather-lane, and saw the prisoner with it .
WILLIAM RAWLINSON ( Police-constable G 54). The prisoner was brought to the station - I asked if knew any thing about the meat ; he said he did not, but it was another boy he had formerly been with .
HENRY HALL. I saw it in his possession - he threw it down ; I ran and took him - when I got back to our shop I found it there.
Prisoner. I saw a boy throw it down , and I took it up .
GUILTY. Aged 12. - Transported for Seven Years.
